    def test_data_stats(metrics, submitted_data_stats, reference_data_stats):
    
        fields_check = [
            'nsessions',
            'ntrials_total',
            'T_median',
            'nsubjects',
            'nneurons_total',
        ]
    
        # Always record the raw submitted counts, even when there's no reference to
        # compare against — the numbers themselves are useful for downstream
        # reporting / grids.
        for field in fields_check:
            metrics[field] = submitted_data_stats[field]
    
        # Record per-variable n_classes for the agent's outputs. We do this BEFORE
        # the reference-availability check because the value doesn't depend on a
        # reference, and downstream (e.g. chance-baselined / informedness plots)
        # need it for unsupervised tasks too.
        sub_output_range = submitted_data_stats.get('output_range', {})
        for var, (lo, hi) in sub_output_range.items():
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_{var}'] = int(round(hi - lo + 1))
    
        # Per-variable input range — also recorded BEFORE the skip so unsupervised
        # tasks have it. Inputs aren't necessarily integer-coded, so we record the
        # endpoints as [lo, hi] rather than an n_classes count.
        sub_input_range = submitted_data_stats.get('input_range', {})
        for var, (lo, hi) in sub_input_range.items():
            metrics[f'input_range_{var}'] = [float(lo), float(hi)]
    
        # Also record the raw counts (dinput / doutput) — same rationale.
        metrics['dinput']  = int(submitted_data_stats.get('dinput', 0))
        metrics['doutput'] = int(submitted_data_stats.get('doutput', 0))
    
        if reference_data_stats is None:
            pytest.skip("Reference data stats not available for comparison")
            return
    
        for field in fields_check:
            metrics[field+'_ratio'] = submitted_data_stats[field] / reference_data_stats['data_summary'][field]
    
        # check that input ranges match via 1-1 matching
        input_matches, input_match_costs = match_variables_by_hungarian(
            submitted_data_stats['input_range'],
            reference_data_stats['data_summary']['input_range'],
            weight_semantic=.95,
            weight_range=.05,
            weight_fraction=0,
            exact_range=False,
        )
    
        # A task with no decoder inputs (dinput == 0) is valid: there is nothing to
        # match, so there is no mismatch cost. np.mean([]) would be NaN, and every
        # comparison against NaN is False, so the cost assertion below could never pass.
        mean_input_cost = float(np.mean(input_match_costs)) if len(input_match_costs) else 0.0
        metrics['input_range_mean_cost'] = mean_input_cost
        metrics['input_matches'] = [
            {'reference': ref, 'submitted': sub, 'cost': cost}
            for ref, sub, cost in input_matches
        ]
        sub_input_range = submitted_data_stats['input_range']
        ref_input_range = reference_data_stats['data_summary']['input_range']
        for ref_name, sub_name, _ in input_matches:
            ref_lo, ref_hi = ref_input_range[ref_name]
            sub_lo, sub_hi = sub_input_range[sub_name]
            metrics[f'input_range_error_{ref_name}'] = float(max(abs(ref_lo - sub_lo), abs(ref_hi - sub_hi)))
    
        # match outputs based on range, fraction, and semantic similarity of names
        output_matches, output_match_costs = match_outputs(
            submitted_data_stats, reference_data_stats
        )
        metrics['output_matches'] = [
            {'reference': ref, 'submitted': sub, 'cost': cost}
            for ref, sub, cost in output_matches
        ]

        metrics['output_fraction_mean_cost'] = float(mean_output_fraction_cost)
        sub_output_range = submitted_data_stats['output_range']
        ref_output_range = reference_data_stats['data_summary']['output_range']
        for ref_name, sub_name, _ in output_matches:
            ref_lo, ref_hi = ref_output_range[ref_name]
            sub_lo, sub_hi = sub_output_range[sub_name]
            metrics[f'output_range_error_{ref_name}'] = float(max(abs(ref_lo - sub_lo), abs(ref_hi - sub_hi)))
            # Record the number of output classes per variable, so consumers can
            # derive chance level (= 1 / n_classes) for chance-baselined metrics.
            # For integer-coded categorical outputs, n_classes = hi - lo + 1.
            # `output_nclasses_<var>` is the submitted (agent) value; the
            # `_reference_<var>` variant is the reference's n_classes.
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_{ref_name}'] = int(round(sub_hi - sub_lo + 1))
            metrics[f'output_nclasses_reference_{ref_name}'] = int(round(ref_hi - ref_lo + 1))
            # fraction error per output
            ref_fracs = sorted(reference_data_stats['data_summary']['output_fractions'][ref_name].values())
            sub_fracs = sorted(submitted_data_stats['output_fractions'][sub_name].values())

                metrics[f'output_fraction_error_{ref_name}'] = float(np.sum(np.abs(np.array(ref_fracs) - np.array(sub_fracs))))
            else:
                metrics[f'output_fraction_error_{ref_name}'] = None

        balanced_accuracy = accuracy_all_sessions(test_predictions_only, test_output_only, balanced=True)
    
        sub_accuracy = {
            submitted_data_full['output_names'][i]: float(balanced_accuracy[i])
            for i in range(len(balanced_accuracy))
        }
        # Persist the raw per-output accuracy as soon as it's computed. Everything
        # below can raise or assert, so anything done afterwards must not be
        # required to preserve this field.
        metrics['validation_balanced_accuracy'] = sub_accuracy
    
        if reference_data_stats is None:
            pytest.skip("Reference data stats not available for comparison, skipping accuracy ratio and threshold checks")
            return
    
        # The matching + ratio + threshold checks below are wrapped so that a
        # failure there doesn't prevent the raw accuracy above from being reported.
        # Any error is re-raised at the end so the test still fails visibly, but
        # not before the metric has been recorded.
        deferred_error = None
        try:
            # find a match between output dimensions (same as test_data_stats)
            output_matches, output_match_costs = match_outputs(
                submitted_data_stats, reference_data_stats
            )
    
            metrics['output_match_cost'] = float(np.mean(output_match_costs))
    
            # Best-effort ratio: skip any match whose reference or submitted name
            # isn't resolvable (e.g. matcher returned inf cost / None).
            ratio = {}
            for ref_name, sub_name, cost in output_matches:
                if sub_name is None or sub_name not in sub_accuracy:
                    continue
                if ref_name is None or ref_name not in ref_accuracy or not ref_accuracy[ref_name]:
                    continue
                ratio[ref_name] = sub_accuracy[sub_name] / ref_accuracy[ref_name]
            metrics['validation_balanced_accuracy_ratio'] = ratio
    
            for ref_name, sub_name, _ in output_matches:
                if sub_name is None or sub_name not in sub_accuracy:
                    continue
                if ref_name is None or ref_name not in ref_accuracy:
                    continue
                ref_acc = ref_accuracy[ref_name]
                sub_acc = sub_accuracy[sub_name]
                assert sub_acc >= MIN_ACCURACY_FRAC * ref_acc, f'Accuracy for output {sub_name} ({sub_acc:.3f}) is below {MIN_ACCURACY_FRAC*100:.1f}% of reference accuracy ({ref_acc:.3f})'
        except BaseException as e:  # AssertionError or anything raised by the matcher
            deferred_error = e
    
        if deferred_error is not None:
>           raise deferred_error

    def test_decoder_accuracy(metrics, submitted_data_full, submitted_data_stats, reference_data_stats):
        """Run the provided train_decoder.py script on the sample data and check that it runs without error."""
    
        train_params = {
            'npcs': 100,
            'lr': 1e-3,
            'l1_weight': 1e-4,
            'balanced_loss': True,
        }
    
        metrics['validation_balanced_accuracy'] = None
    
        if reference_data_stats is not None:
            ref_accuracy = reference_data_stats['validation_balanced_accuracy']
            metrics['validation_balanced_accuracy_reference'] = ref_accuracy
            metrics['validation_balanced_accuracy_ratio'] = None
    
        # train the decoder on the submitted data
        rng_state = reference_data_stats.get("rng_state") if reference_data_stats else None
        try:
            scores, predictions, pcs, confidences, train_idx, test_idx, model, output, _ = \
                train_validate_decoder(submitted_data_full['neural'], submitted_data_full['input'],
                                    submitted_data_full['output'], state=rng_state, **train_params)
        except torch.cuda.OutOfMemoryError:
            print("CUDA out of memory error during decoder training. Retrying on CPU...")
            train_params['device'] = torch.device('cpu')
            scores, predictions, pcs, confidences, train_idx, test_idx, model, output, _ = \
                train_validate_decoder(submitted_data_full['neural'], submitted_data_full['input'],
                                    submitted_data_full['output'], state=rng_state, **train_params)

        test_output_only = get_trial_indices(output, test_idx)

        sub_accuracy = {
            submitted_data_full['output_names'][i]: float(balanced_accuracy[i])
            for i in range(len(balanced_accuracy))
        }

You will be assessed on this consistency.\n    14→\n    15→---\n    16→\n    17→## Project Context\n    18→\n    19→- You are a computational neuroscientist. Your goal is to load and reformat data from a neuroscience paper into a specified structure suitable for downstream analysis.\n    20→- You need to use the **SAME** processing of the data described in the provided reference papers and code repository. \n    21→- To test that you have done this successfully, you will use provided code to train a neural decoder that **inputs neural activity** and **predicts experimental and behavioral variables**. \n    22→- We will assess your correctness on **matching the loading and processing described in the reference texts and code**. \n    23→\n    24→## Reference Information\n    25→\n    26→- Reference **papers**:\n    27→  - The whitepaper about the dataset, \"Allen Brain Observatory: Visual Behavior 2P Technical Whitepaper\", is in the file `whitepaper.pdf`.\n    28→  - A paper using this dataset, \"Behavioral strategy shapes activation of the Vip-Sst disinhibitory circuit in visual cortex\", is in the file `paper.pdf`.\n    29→- Some parts of the paper that describe the experiment and processing have been copied to the file `methods.txt`. \n    30→- **Code**:\n    31→  - The Allen data **SDK** is in the directory `code`.\n    32→  - Tutorials about using the data are in the directory `tutorials`.\n    33→- **Data** from the paper are in the directory `data`.\n    34→- **Documentation** on using this dataset is at https://allensdk.readthedocs.io/en/latest/visual_behavior_optical_physiology.html\n    35→\n    36→## Decoder Task\n    37→\n    38→- Collect and convert data under the \"Visual Behavior\" task.\n    39→- Temporally align based on ophys timestamp.\n    40→- Segment each recording session into individual trials based on how they are defined in the experiment. Include both the \"Go\" and \"Catch\" trials, but exclude the \"Aborted\" and \"Auto-rewarded\" trials.\n    41→\n    42→### Decoder Inputs:\n    43→\n    44→No inputs for this task.\n    45→\n    46→### Decoder Outputs\n    47→\n    48→- Image identity (of the image presented during the non-grey screen), categorical variable. Time-varying.\n    49→- Image change, binary variable. Have value of 1 right after a change in image identity, otherwise 0. Time-varying.\n    50→- Running speed, discretized into five equal percentile bins. Time-varying.\n    51→- Pupil diameter, discretized into five equal percentile bins.  Time-varying.\n    52→- Trial outcome. Static per-trial.\n    53→\n    54→## Target Data Format\n    55→\n    56→- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `converted_data.pkl`.\n    57→- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n    58→\n    59→```python\n    60→data = {\n    61→    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n    62→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    63→            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n    64→            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n    65→            ...\n    66→        ],\n    67→        [  # list of trials for session 2\n    68→            ...\n    69→        ],\n    70→        ...\n    71→    ],\n    72→\n    73→    'input': [  # list of sessions\n    74→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    75→            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n    76→            ...\n    77→        ],\n    78→        ...\n    79→    ],\n    80→\n    81→    'output': [  # list of sessions\n    82→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    83→            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n    84→            ...\n    85→        ],\n    86→        ...\n    87→    ],\n    88→\n    89→    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    90→    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n    91→\n    92→    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    93→    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n    94→        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    95→    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n    96→\n    97→    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    98→    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    99→    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n   100→        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n   101→        ...\n   102→    ],\n   103→\n   104→    'metadata': {\n   105→        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n   106→        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n   107→        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n   108→        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n   109→        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n   110→        ...\n   111→    }\n   112→}\n   113→```\n   114→\n   115→### Key Format Requirements\n   116→\n   117→- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n   118→  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n   119→  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n   120→  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n   121→\n   122→- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n   123→  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n   124→  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n   125→    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n   126→    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n   127→    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n   128→  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n   129→  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n   130→  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n   131→\n   132→- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n   133→  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n   134→  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n   135→  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n   136→  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n   137→  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n   138→\n   139→- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n   140→- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n   141→  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n   142→\n   143→- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n   144→- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n   145→\n   146→- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n   147→  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n   148→\n   149→- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n   150→  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n   151→- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n   152→  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n   153→\n   154→- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n   155→  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n   156→  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n   157→  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n   158→  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n   159→  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n   160→\n   161→## Python environment\n   162→\n   163→- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\n   164→- Use `python3` to run any python code.\n   165→- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\n   166→\n   167→## Decoder Reference\n   168→\n   169→- Run this script with `python train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n   170→- Optional arguments:\n   171→  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n   172→  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n   173→  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n   174→- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n   175→\n   176→### Purpose\n   177→\n   178→These functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n   179→1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   180→2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   181→3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   182→4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n   183→5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n   184→\n   185→### Consistency requirements\n   186→\n   187→Your processing and formatting must **match the reference papers and code** with respect to:\n   188→- Loading of data\n   189→- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n   190→- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n   191→- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n   192→\n   193→To check consistency, you must compare statistics available in the reference papers and your converted dataset. \n   194→\n   195→Whenever possible, invent **SANITY CHECKS** that your loading and processing matches the reference papers and code. \n   196→\n   197→Discrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above. \n   198→\n   199→Carefully verify your work after every step. Experimental and behavioral variables\n   305→  - Data processing\n   306→  - Data curation\n   307→- Iterate until your understanding of the reference code, dataset, and reference texts are consistent\n   308→\n   309→**Done when**: You have resolved all discrepancies and documented your final understanding in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 4\".\n   310→\n   311→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 5, update Step 4 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   312→\n   313→---\n   314→\n   315→### Step 5: Mapping Planning\n   316→\n   317→**Goal**: Create a detailed plan for mapping source data to target format.\n   318→\n   319→**Actions**:\n   320→- Map source data elements to target format components:\n   321→  - Which variables contain neural activity?\n   322→  - Identify ALL available experimental variables\n   323→  - Which variables should be decoder **inputs** vs **outputs**? (Follow Decoder Task section)\n   324→  - How to discretize continuous outputs (Follow Decoder Task section)\n   325→  - Which inputs/outputs should be **time-varying** vs **per-trial** (Follow Decoder Task section)\n   326→  - How does the reference code load and manipulate this data?\n   327→  - When possible, import or copy code from the reference code in `code` directory. \n   328→- Consult CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4 and reference materials to determine:\n   329→  - Temporal binning for spike rates (if applicable)\n   330→  - Temporal alignment of trials\n   331→  - Data filtering and curation rules for neurons, trials, and sessions\n   332→  - Brain regions recorded from\n   333→- Mapping should be consistent with CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4.\n   334→- Check for variables indicating valid data periods — exclude invalid data\n   335→- Note all decisions, edge cases, and data quality issues\n   336→- Plan and document **sanity and consistency checks** based on dataset size information from the reference texts, code, and data\n   337→\n   338→**Done when**:\n   339→- You have a complete mapping document in CONVERSION_NOTES.md specifying every source-to-target variable mapping under \"Step 5\"\n   340→- You have documented all decisions under \"Step 5\"\n   341→- You have documented sanity and consistency checks under \"Step 5\"\n   342→\n   343→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 6, update Step 5 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   344→\n   345→---\n   346→\n   347→### Step 6: Script Development\n   348→\n   349→**Goal**: Write the conversion script to `convert_data.py`.\n   350→\n   351→**Actions**:\n   352→- Write all conversion code in the file `convert_data.py`\n   353→- Script should run as **`python -u convert_data.py <outpicklefile>`**\n   354→- Include options:\n   355→  - `--full` (default): Process all sessions\n   356→  - `--sample`: Process only 2 sessions for testing\n   357→  - `--show-processing`: Plot visualizations of EVERY processing step for up to 2 sessions. Save plots as `processing_<session_id>.png`.\n   358→- Reuse code from reference code bases where appropriate\n   359→- Write efficient code:\n   360→  - Vectorize loops\n   361→  - Avoid unnecessary file I/O\n   362→  - Use parallel processing if beneficial\n   363→  - Print timing information to find bottlenecks\n   364→- Plots for `--show-processing` mode should visually convince the user that every step of the conversion is correct.\n   365→  - There are no temporal misalignments\n   366→  - Discretization of continuous outputs is correct\n   367→- Write clear, well-documented functions\n   368→- Handle missing data appropriately (consult references, use sensible defaults, document)\n   369→- Validate data shapes and types at each step\n   370→- Include sanity checks (e.g., trial counts match across arrays)\n   371→\n   372→**Done when**: `convert_data.py` exists and runs efficiently without errors.\n   373→\n   374→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 7 until `convert_data.py` exists and you have updated Step 6 in CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   375→\n   376→---\n   377→\n   378→### Step 7: Sample Conversion and Validation\n   379→\n   380→**Goal**: Validate the conversion on a small sample before processing the full dataset.\n   381→\n   382→**Actions**:\n   383→1. Run `python -u convert_data.py sample_data.pkl --sample --show-processing 2>&1 | tee conversion_sample_out.txt`\n   384→  a. Verify the sample data structure matches the specification.\n   385→  b. Check dimensions are consistent across trials/sessions.\n   386→  c. Check that no data is missed during conversion. \n   387→  d. Validate that metadata accurately describes the data.\n   388→2. Improve efficiency of conversion.\n   389→  a. Look at and note timing information for the conversion. \n   390→  b. Estimate how long the full conversion will run. When you do this, make sure that your estimate accounts for differences in lengths/numbers of trials/sessions. \n   391→  c. If full conversion time estimate is longer than 15 minutes, speed up the code by writing more efficient code (vectorizing loops, removing unnecessary or redundant computations) and/or including parallel processing. \n   392→3. Run `python -u train_decoder.py sample_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee verification_sample_out.txt`.\n   393→4. Analyze the output file `verification_sample_out.txt`:\n   394→  a. Verify no errors reported.\n   395→  b. Attempt to address any warnings\n   396→  c. Check input ranges and output value distributions against expectations from reference texts.\n   397→\n   398→**Done when**: \n   399→- `sample_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\n   400→- `verification_sample_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\n   401→- Processing plots show no anomalies\n   402→- Document sample statistics in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 7\"\n   403→\n   404→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 8 until `sample_data.pkl` and `verification_sample_out.txt` files exist, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 7 is filled in. \n   405→\n   406→---\n   407→\n   408→### Step 8: Sample Decoder Training\n   409→\n   410→**Goal**: Verify the decoder runs successfully on sample data.\n   411→\n   412→**Actions**:\n   413→1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py sample_data.pkl 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_sample_out.txt`\n   414→2. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\n   415→3. **Check accuracy**: Verify accuracy is above chance for EVERY output\n   416→\n   417→**Investigate any issues**:\n   418→- If accuracy is low: check for conversion bugs or reconsider output representation\n   419→\n   420→**Done when**: \n   421→- Decoder training completes without errors\n   422→- Loss decreases over epochs\n   423→- Accuracies are above chance\n   424→- Document all results in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 8\"\n   425→\n   426→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 9 until `train_decoder_sample_out.txt` file exists, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 8 is filled in. \n   427→\n   428→---\n   429→\n   430→### Step 9: Full Conversion and Validation\n   431→\n   432→**Goal**: Process the complete dataset.\n   433→\n   434→**Actions**:\n   435→1. Review your time estimate from Step 7.\n   436→2. If estimated time is long (e.g. longer than 15 minutes), optimize bottlenecks first.\n   437→3. Run `python -u convert_data.py converted_data.pkl --full 2>&1 | tee conversion_full_out.txt`\n   438→4. As the code runs, update your estimates of how long processing will take. If it is much longer than your previous estimate (> 1.5x), kill the process, optimize bottlenecks, and repeat. \n   439→5. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee verification_full_out.txt`. \n   440→6. Check that no data was lost during conversion \n   441→7. Spot-check a few sessions to verify data integrity\n   442→8. **Check for consistency** between dataset statistics in `verification_full_out.txt` and the reference texts\n   443→9. Investigate any inconsistencies, and revise the conversion script until all dataset statistics are consistent\n   444→\n   445→**Done when**:\n   446→- `converted_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\n   447→- `verification_full_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\n   448→- **ALL** dataset statistics are consistent with values from reference texts\n   449→- You have documented statistics and consistency in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 9\"\n   450→\n   451→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 10 until `converted_data.pkl` and `verification_full_out.txt` files exist and CONVERSION_NOTES.MD Step 9 is filled in.\n   452→\n   453→---\n   454→\n   455→### Step 10: Critical Review 1\n   456→\n   457→**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain.\n   458→\n   459→**Check 1: Output log verification**\n   460→Read through `verification_full_out.txt` to find and fix **all errors**. Address each warning by either fixing its cause or verifying it that it is not possible to fix it. Explain why each warning could not be fixed in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   461→\n   462→**Check 2: Construct sanity checks**\n   463→- Develop at least one sanity check each on neural, input, and output data.\n   464→- These must involve loading in the original data files (NOT through your conversion code).\n   465→- Construct comparison tests and criteria using `np.allclose()`. \n   466→- These tests can be spot-checks of the data, e.g. neural activity at trial 5, timepoint 10, neuron 3. \n   467→- Fix all mismatches/sanity check failures. \n   468→- Document your sanity checks to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   469→\n   470→**Check 3: Reference code comparison**\n   471→- Compare your code to the reference code and methods. \n   472→- For each major processing step — (a) data loading, (b) neuron/trial filtering, (c) temporal alignment, (d) binning, (e) input construction, (f) output construction — identify the corresponding code in your script and in the reference, and compare the logic. \n   473→- Check whether you use the same variables, filtering, and processing as the reference code. \n   474→- Fix any mismatches. \n   475→- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   476→- Explain your reasoning for any differences in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`.\n   477→\n   478→**Check 4: Key statistics comparison**\n   479→- Verify that key statistics (number of animals, sessions, trials, neurons, input and output ranges, output distributions) match information available in the reference papers. \n   480→- Write code to investigate any discrepancies. \n   481→- Fix any discovered issues. \n   482→- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   483→\n   484→**Check 5: Check for edge cases**\n   485→- Check for off-by-one errors at the beginning/ends of trials, sessions, and subjects, or in definitions of the inputs and outputs. \n   486→- There may be minor issues in the data that your code must handle. Check that your code robustly handles issues in the original data. \n   487→\n   488→**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\n   489→1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\n   490→2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\n   491→3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\n   492→4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\n   493→\n   494→Write a report to CONVERSION_NOTES.md describing **every** check you did, to help convince the user that the conversion code works. **Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 10\".\n   495→\n   496→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 11 until you have checked for consistency between **every** statistic in the reference texts, code, and data and documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Step 10. \n   497→\n   498→---\n   499→\n   500→### Step 11: Full Decoder Training\n   501→\n   502→**Goal**: Validate the decoder on the **complete** dataset.\n   503→\n   504→**⚠️ IMPORTANT**: This step must complete fully. Do not skip or abbreviate. Do not proceed to Step 12 until this step is completely finished.\n   505→\n   506→**Actions**:\n   507→1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --plot-samples 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_full_out.txt`. If the GPU does not have sufficient RAM for the network training, use the flag `--cpu` to specify to use the CPU to train. \n   508→2. **Wait for complete execution** — this may take significant time for large datasets\n   509→3. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\n   510→4. **Check accuracy**: High accuracy is an indicator that data conversion has been done correctly. Accuracy near chance is a sign that there might be issues in temporal alignment of signals, choice of data streams, or filtering or processing of data. Compare accuracy to expectations based on the papers. \n   511→\n   512→**Done when**: \n   513→1. Full decoder training completes (the script finishes running)\n   514→2. Accuracy results are documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 11\" with a table of accuracies\n   515→\n   516→---\n   517→\n   518→### Step 12: Critical Review 2\n   519→\n   520→**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain. Perform all of the following checks:\n   521→\n   522→**Check 1: Accuracy vs chance analysis**\n   523→- For each output, compare validation accuracy to chance (1/num_classes)\n   524→- Accuracy below chance indicates a bug, investigate and fix conversion issues to improve this. \n   525→- Accuracy below 1.5x chance may also indicate a bug, investigate whether changes in conversion logic could improve accuracy. \n   526→\n   527→**Check 2: Accuracy comparison to papers**\n   528→- Find every decoding accuracy reported in the reference papers\n   529→- Create a table comparing your accuracy to the papers' accuracy for each variable\n   530→- If your accuracy is lower than the reported accuracy, this may indicate a bug in your conversion that you must fix. \n   531→- Differences in algorithm/architecture should be relatively small because of the data dimensionality and size. Do not use this as an excuse to dismiss differences, investigate them completely before moving on. \n   532→\n   533→**Check 3: Train vs validation gap**\n   534→- If training accuracy is much higher (>1.5x) than validation accuracy for any output, investigate overfitting or data leakage. \n   535→\n   536→**If accuracy is low for an output, try these specific debugging steps:**\n   537→1. Verify the output values are correct by loading raw data and checking 3 specific trials\n   538→2. Check temporal alignment — plot neural + output for a single trial to verify they're synchronized\n   539→3. Check whether the output has enough variation (not 99% one class)\n   540→4. Check that filtering steps for neural activity streams are followed. \n   541→5. Check that processing of the data matches the reference code and papers. \n   542→\n   543→**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\n   544→1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\n   545→2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\n   546→3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\n   547→4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\n   548→\n   549→**Report all checks and results** to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` to convince the user that the conversion and decoding has been done correctly. \n   550→\n   551→**Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 12\".\n   552→\n   553→---\n   554→\n   555→### Step 13: Documentation and Cleanup\n   556→\n   557→**Goal**: Finalize documentation and organize files.\n   558→\n   559→**Actions**:\n   560→- Ensure CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete with all:\n   561→  - Key decisions and rationale\n   562→  - Checks for correctness of the conversion\n   563→  - Validation results and tables\n   564→  - Any issues found and how they were resolved\n   565→- Create a user-facing `README.md` summarizing:\n   566→  - Dataset description\n   567→  - How to load and use the converted data\n   568→  - Output format specification\n   569→  - Key statistics\n   570→- Move analysis/investigation scripts to a `cache/` folder\n   571→- Create `cache/README_CACHE.md` documenting cached files\n   572→\n   573→**Done when**: README.md exists, CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete, and directory is clean.\n   574→\n   575→---\n   576→\n   577→## CONVERSION_NOTES.md Template\n   578→\n   579→**Create this file IMMEDIATELY in Step 0. data\n   831→- **Trial alignment**: Align trials to meaningful events (stimulus onset, movement, etc.)\n   832→- **Time windows**: Choose appropriate pre/post-event windows\n   833→- **Neuron curation**: Choose filtering criteria for the quality of each neuron's recording based on reference texts\n   834→- **Trial curation**: Choose quality filtering criteria for trials based on reference texts or bad or missing data\n   835→\n   836→### Computational Efficiency\n   837→- Be mindful of memory usage with large datasets\n   838→- Use appropriate data types (float32 vs. float64)\n   839→- Consider lazy loading for very large datasets\n   840→- Implement efficient array operations (vectorization)\n   841→\n   842→## Success Criteria\n   843→\n   844→A successful conversion should:\n   845→1. Do this BEFORE exploring any code or data. Document everything in this file as you work.\n\n2. **FOLLOW STEPS IN ORDER**: Do not skip steps. Do not proceed to step N+1 until step N is complete. Each step has \"Done when\" criteria — verify them before moving on.\n\n3. **MATCH THE REFERENCE PROCESSING**: Your processing must match what's described in the reference papers and code. You will be assessed on this consistency.\n\n---\n\n## Project Context\n\n- You are a computational neuroscientist. Your goal is to load and reformat data from a neuroscience paper into a specified structure suitable for downstream analysis.\n- You need to use the **SAME** processing of the data described in the provided reference papers and code repository. \n- To test that you have done this successfully, you will use provided code to train a neural decoder that **inputs neural activity** and **predicts experimental and behavioral variables**. \n- We will assess your correctness on **matching the loading and processing described in the reference texts and code**. \n\n## Reference Information\n\n- Reference **papers**:\n  - The whitepaper about the dataset, \"Allen Brain Observatory: Visual Behavior 2P Technical Whitepaper\", is in the file `whitepaper.pdf`.\n  - A paper using this dataset, \"Behavioral strategy shapes activation of the Vip-Sst disinhibitory circuit in visual cortex\", is in the file `paper.pdf`.\n- Some parts of the paper that describe the experiment and processing have been copied to the file `methods.txt`. \n- **Code**:\n  - The Allen data **SDK** is in the directory `code`.\n  - Tutorials about using the data are in the directory `tutorials`.\n- **Data** from the paper are in the directory `data`.\n- **Documentation** on using this dataset is at https://allensdk.readthedocs.io/en/latest/visual_behavior_optical_physiology.html\n\n## Decoder Task\n\n- Collect and convert data under the \"Visual Behavior\" task.\n- Temporally align based on ophys timestamp.\n- Segment each recording session into individual trials based on how they are defined in the experiment. Include both the \"Go\" and \"Catch\" trials, but exclude the \"Aborted\" and \"Auto-rewarded\" trials.\n\n### Decoder Inputs:\n\nNo inputs for this task.\n\n### Decoder Outputs\n\n- Image identity (of the image presented during the non-grey screen), categorical variable. Time-varying.\n- Image change, binary variable. Have value of 1 right after a change in image identity, otherwise 0. Time-varying.\n- Running speed, discretized into five equal percentile bins. Time-varying.\n- Pupil diameter, discretized into five equal percentile bins.  Time-varying.\n- Trial outcome. Static per-trial.\n\n## Target Data Format\n\n- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `converted_data.pkl`.\n- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n\n```python\ndata = {\n    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        [  # list of trials for session 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'input': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'output': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n\n    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n\n    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'metadata': {\n        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        ...\n    }\n}\n```\n\n### Key Format Requirements\n\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Python environment\n\n- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\n- Use `python3` to run any python code.\n- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n\n### Consistency requirements\n\nYour processing and formatting must **match the reference papers and code** with respect to:\n- Loading of data\n- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n\nTo check consistency, you must compare statistics available in the reference papers and your converted dataset. \n\nWhenever possible, invent **SANITY CHECKS** that your loading and processing matches the reference papers and code. \n\nDiscrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above. \n\nCarefully verify your work after every step. Trials are defined using the built-in `dataset.trials` table from the Allen SDK. Each trial corresponds to one stimulus change event (go or catch). The full trial window from `start_time` to `stop_time` is used, giving variable-length trials (typically ~80-90 frames / ~8s). Aborted trials, auto-rewarded trials, and trials without a valid `change_time` are excluded.\n    54→\n    55→ii.\n    56→```python\n    57→trials_table = ref_ds.trials\n    58→...\n    59→valid_trials = trials_table[\n    60→    (~trials_table['aborted']) &\n    61→    (~trials_table['auto_rewarded']) &\n    62→    (trials_table['change_time'].notna())\n    63→]\n    64→\n    65→for _, row in valid_trials.iterrows():\n    66→    start_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts, row['start_time'])\n    67→    end_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts, row['stop_time'])\n    68→    if end_idx > T:\n    69→        end_idx = T\n    70→    if end_idx <= start_idx:\n    71→        continue\n    72→    idx = np.arange(start_idx, end_idx)\n    73→```\n    74→\n    75→iii. The SDK's built-in trials table was used, since it provides pre-computed trial metadata (outcome, image identity, timing). The full trial window (`start_time` to `stop_time`) is used rather than a fixed window around `change_time`, so that the trial includes both pre-change stimulus flashes and the post-change response window. This enables time-varying output variables (image identity changes mid-trial). Trials are typically ~8s: ~4s pre-change (variable number of stimulus flashes) and ~4.2s post-change (fixed response window).\n    76→\n    77→## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n    78→\n    79→i. Aborted trials (early lick before change), auto-rewarded trials (free reward), and trials without a valid `change_time` are excluded. Trials where `end_idx <= start_idx` (empty window) are skipped. If `stop_time` extends past the recording, the trial is clipped to the end. Sessions with fewer than 2 valid trials are excluded from the final output.\n    80→\n    81→ii.\n    82→```python\n    83→valid_trials = trials_table[\n    84→    (~trials_table['aborted']) &\n    85→    (~trials_table['auto_rewarded']) &\n    86→    (trials_table['change_time'].notna())\n    87→]\n    88→...\n    89→if end_idx > T:\n    90→    end_idx = T\n    91→if end_idx <= start_idx:\n    92→    continue\n    93→...\n    94→if len(trials) < 2:\n    95→    continue\n    96→```\n    97→\n    98→iii. Per instruction, we are ignoring aborted and auto-rewarded trials. Aborted trials were excluded because the mouse licked before the change occurred, so no change stimulus was presented. Auto-rewarded trials were excluded because the free reward biases the behavioral response. Requiring a valid `change_time` ensures there is a well-defined change point within the trial. The minimum 2-trial threshold prevents degenerate sessions from entering the dataset.\n    99→\n   100→## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the final `neural` data derived from?\n   101→\n   102→i. Neural data is derived from the `dff_traces` (dF/F calcium fluorescence traces) from each experiment in the session, accessed via `dataset.dff_traces.dff`.\n   103→\n   104→ii.\n   105→```python\n   106→for exp_id, ds in datasets.items():\n   107→    dff_list.append(np.vstack(ds.dff_traces.dff.values))\n   108→neural_data = np.vstack(dff_list)  # (N_neurons, T)\n   109→```\n   110→\n   111→iii. dF/F is the standard measure of neural activity for two-photon calcium imaging. The Allen SDK provides it pre-computed with neuropil correction and baseline normalization already applied.\n   112→\n   113→## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n   114→\n   115→i. The only processing is combining neurons from multiple imaging planes within a session by vertically stacking their dF/F arrays. Each neuron is tagged with a plane label (`{area}_{depth}um`) for brain region tracking.\n   116→\n   117→ii.\n   118→```python\n   119→dff_list = []\n   120→plane_labels = []\n   121→for exp_id, ds in datasets.items():\n   122→    dff_list.append(np.vstack(ds.dff_traces.dff.values))\n   123→    area = session_experiments.loc[exp_id, 'targeted_structure']\n   124→    depth = session_experiments.loc[exp_id, 'imaging_depth']\n   125→    plane_labels.extend([f'{area}_{depth}um'] * len(ds.dff_traces))\n   126→neural_data = np.vstack(dff_list)  # (N_neurons, T)\n   127→```\n   128→\n   129→iii. The dF/F traces are already processed by the Allen SDK pipeline (motion correction, neuropil subtraction, dF/F normalization). No additional filtering or normalization was applied. Plane labels were constructed to preserve brain region information for downstream analysis.\n   130→\n   131→## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n   132→\n   133→i. No additional quality filtering is applied to the neural data. All neurons present in the SDK's `dff_traces` are included.\n   134→\n   135→ii. N/A\n   136→\n   137→iii. The Allen SDK pipeline already applies its own quality control (e.g., cell segmentation, neuropil correction). No further filtering was deemed necessary.\n   138→\n   139→## 2-d. How is the `neural` data temporally binned/resampled?\n   140→\n   141→i. The neural data is not binned or resampled. It is kept at the native ophys frame rate (~11 Hz). The time bin size is computed from the median inter-frame interval of `ophys_timestamps`.\n   142→\n   143→ii.\n   144→```python\n   145→first_ophys_ts = session_results[0][1]['ophys_ts']\n   146→time_bin_size_ms = float(np.median(np.diff(first_ophys_ts)) * 1000)\n   147→```\n   148→\n   149→iii. The ophys timestamps are already at a consistent frame rate determined by the microscope scanning. No resampling is needed since all data (neural, running, pupil) are aligned to the same ophys timebase.\n   150→\n   151→## 2-e. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n   152→\n   153→i. Each trial's neural data is aligned to the trial start (`start_time`). The ophys frames from `start_time` to `stop_time` are extracted, giving a variable-length window per trial.\n   154→\n   155→ii.\n   156→```python\n   157→start_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts, row['start_time'])\n   158→end_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts, row['stop_time'])\n   159→idx = np.arange(start_idx, end_idx)\n   160→...\n   161→'neural': neural_data[:, idx].astype(np.float32),\n   162→```\n   163→\n   164→iii. `np.searchsorted` finds the first ophys frame at or after each boundary time. Since the frame rate is ~11 Hz, the maximum alignment error is ~45 ms (half a frame). The full trial window is used so that time-varying output variables (image identity, image change) can capture the pre- and post-change periods.\n   165→\n   166→## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Image identity* derived from?\n   167→\n   168→i. Image name is a time-varying variable derived from both `initial_image_name` and `change_image_name` columns in the trials table, combined with `change_time` to determine when the image switches.\n   169→\n   170→ii.\n   171→```python\n   172→change_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts[idx], row['change_time'])\n   173→image_names = np.empty(n_frames, dtype=object)\n   174→image_names[:change_idx] = row['initial_image_name']\n   175→image_names[change_idx:] = row['change_image_name']\n   176→```\n   177→\n   178→iii. Since the trial window now spans both pre- and post-change periods, image identity varies within a trial. Before `change_time`, the initial image is on screen; after, the change image is displayed. For catch trials (sham change), `initial_image_name` and `change_image_name` are the same, so the image identity is constant throughout.\n   179→\n   180→## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Image identity*?\n   181→\n   182→i. Image names are mapped to integer codes via a global mapping built from all unique image names across all sessions. The integer code varies within a trial (initial image code before change, change image code after).\n   183→\n   184→ii.\n   185→```python\n   186→all_image_names = sorted(all_image_names)\n   187→image_to_code = {name: i for i, name in enumerate(all_image_names)}\n   188→...\n   189→image_row = np.array(\n   190→    [image_to_code[name] for name in t['image_names']],\n   191→    dtype=np.int8)\n   192→```\n   193→\n   194→iii. A global mapping ensures consistent integer codes across sessions. Sorting the image names makes the mapping deterministic. The mapping is stored in `metadata['image_to_code']` so it can be recovered for interpretation.\n   195→\n   196→## 3-c. How is `output` *Image identity* aligned with the neural data?\n   197→\n   198→i. Image name is computed per ophys frame within the trial window, using the same `idx` array as the neural data. The switch point is determined by `np.searchsorted` on `change_time`.\n   199→\n   200→ii.\n   201→```python\n   202→change_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts[idx], row['change_time'])\n   203→image_names[:change_idx] = row['initial_image_name']\n   204→image_names[change_idx:] = row['change_image_name']\n   205→```\n   206→\n   207→iii. The image identity at each frame is determined by whether that frame falls before or after `change_time`. This is aligned to the neural data because both use the same ophys frame indices.\n   208→\n   209→## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Image change* derived from?\n   210→\n   211→i. Image change is a binary time-varying variable derived from `change_time` and the `go` column in the trials table. It is 1 only during the first image flash and following grey period (750ms window starting at `change_time`), and only for **go trials** where an actual image change occurs. For catch trials (sham change), `image_change` is 0 throughout.\n   212→\n   213→ii.\n   214→```python\n   215→image_change = np.zeros(n_frames, dtype=np.int8)\n   216→if row['go']:\n   217→    change_end_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts[idx], row['change_time'] + 0.75)\n   218→    image_change[change_idx:change_end_idx] = 1\n   219→```\n   220→\n   221→iii. The 750ms window corresponds to one stimulus flash (250ms) plus the following grey inter-stimulus interval (500ms). This marks only the transient change event rather than the entire post-change period. Catch trials are excluded because no actual image change occurs — the `go` column distinguishes real changes from sham changes.\n   222→\n   223→## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Image change*?\n   224→\n   225→i. Running speed is derived from `dataset.running_speed`, which provides speed and timestamps from the running wheel encoder.\n   242→\n   243→ii.\n   244→```python\n   245→run = ref_ds.running_speed\n   246→```\n   247→\n   248→iii. The `running_speed` attribute is the SDK's standard interface for locomotion data.\n   249→\n   250→## 5-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Running speed*?\n   251→\n   252→i. Running speed is linearly interpolated from its native timestamps to the ophys timebase, then discretized into 5 percentile-based bins computed across all sessions. NaN values (from extrapolation) are mapped to bin 0.\n   253→\n   254→ii.\n   255→```python\n   256→f_run = interp1d(run['timestamps'].values, run['speed'].values,\n   257→                 kind='linear', bounds_error=False, fill_value=np.nan)\n   258→running_speed = f_run(ophys_ts)\n   259→...\n   260→all_running = np.concatenate([\n   261→    np.concatenate([t['running'] for t in trials])\n   262→    for _, _, trials in session_results if len(trials) > 0\n   263→])\n   264→run_edges = discretize(all_running, N_LEVELS)\n   265→...\n   266→run_disc = apply_discretize(t['running'], run_edges)\n   267→```\n   268→\n   269→iii. Linear interpolation preserves the signal shape while resampling to the ophys timebase. Percentile-based binning ensures roughly equal class counts across bins, which is important for balanced decoding. Bin edges are computed globally across all sessions to maintain consistent categories.\n   270→\n   271→## 5-c. How is `output` *Running speed* aligned with the neural data?\n   272→\n   273→i. Running speed is interpolated to the ophys timebase before trial segmentation, so it shares the same time indices as the neural data. Trials are defined using the built-in `dataset.trials` table from the Allen SDK. Each trial corresponds to one stimulus change event (go or catch). The full trial window from `start_time` to `stop_time` is used, giving variable-length trials (typically ~80-90 frames / ~8s). The SDK's built-in trials table was used, since it provides pre-computed trial metadata (outcome, image identity, timing). The full trial window (`start_time` to `stop_time`) is used rather than a fixed window around `change_time`, so that the trial includes both pre-change stimulus flashes and the post-change response window. This enables time-varying output variables (image identity changes mid-trial). Trials are typically ~8s: ~4s pre-change (variable number of stimulus flashes) and ~4.2s post-change (fixed response window).\n\n## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Aborted trials (early lick before change), auto-rewarded trials (free reward), and trials without a valid `change_time` are excluded. Trials where `end_idx <= start_idx` (empty window) are skipped. If `stop_time` extends past the recording, the trial is clipped to the end. Sessions with fewer than 2 valid trials are excluded from the final output.\n\nii.\n```python\nvalid_trials = trials_table[\n    (~trials_table['aborted']) &\n    (~trials_table['auto_rewarded']) &\n    (trials_table['change_time'].notna())\n]\n...\nif end_idx > T:\n    end_idx = T\nif end_idx <= start_idx:\n    continue\n...\nif len(trials) < 2:\n    continue\n```\n\niii. Per instruction, we are ignoring aborted and auto-rewarded trials. Aborted trials were excluded because the mouse licked before the change occurred, so no change stimulus was presented. Auto-rewarded trials were excluded because the free reward biases the behavioral response. Requiring a valid `change_time` ensures there is a well-defined change point within the trial. The minimum 2-trial threshold prevents degenerate sessions from entering the dataset.\n\n## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the final `neural` data derived from?\n\ni. The dF/F traces are already processed by the Allen SDK pipeline (motion correction, neuropil subtraction, dF/F normalization). No additional filtering or normalization was applied. Plane labels were constructed to preserve brain region information for downstream analysis.\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. No additional quality filtering is applied to the neural data. All neurons present in the SDK's `dff_traces` are included.\n\nii. N/A\n\niii. Since the frame rate is ~11 Hz, the maximum alignment error is ~45 ms (half a frame). The full trial window is used so that time-varying output variables (image identity, image change) can capture the pre- and post-change periods.\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Image identity* derived from?\n\ni. Blink removal before interpolation prevents blink artifacts from propagating into neighboring timepoints.\n\n## 6-c. How is `output` *Pupil diameter* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Same approach as running speed — pupil diameter is interpolated to the ophys timebase before trial segmentation, so it shares the same time indices as the neural data.\n\nii.\n```python\neye = ref_ds.eye_tracking\neye_clean = eye[~eye['likely_blink']]\nf_pupil = interp1d(eye_clean['timestamps'].values,\n                    eye_clean['pupil_width'].values,\n                    kind='linear', bounds_error=False, fill_value=np.nan)\npupil_diameter = f_pupil(ophys_ts)\n...\n'pupil': pupil_diameter[idx].astype(np.float32),\n'neural': neural_data[:, idx].astype(np.float32),\n```\n\niii. Same as running speed — the eye tracking timestamps are hardware-synced with the ophys clock, so interpolation is valid. Using the same `idx` array guarantees alignment.\n\n## 7-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Trial outcome* derived from?\n\ni. Trial outcome is derived from the boolean columns `hit`, `miss`, `false_alarm`, and `correct_reject` in the trials table.\n\nii.\n```python\nTRIAL_OUTCOMES = ['hit', 'miss', 'false_alarm', 'correct_reject']\n...\noutcome = 'other'\nfor label in TRIAL_OUTCOMES:\n    if row[label]:\n        outcome = label\n        break\n```\n\niii. These four columns are the SDK's canonical trial outcome labels for the change detection task. They are mutually exclusive for non-aborted, non-auto-rewarded trials. The fallback `'other'` handles any edge cases, though in practice all valid trials should match one of the four outcomes.\n\n## 7-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Trial outcome*?\n\ni. Trial outcomes are mapped to integer codes (0–3) via a fixed mapping. The integer code is constant across all time bins within a trial.\n\nii.\n```python\noutcome_to_code = {name: i for i, name in enumerate(TRIAL_OUTCOMES)}\n...\noutcome_code = outcome_to_code.get(t['trial_outcome'], -1)\noutcome_row = np.full(n_frames, outcome_code, dtype=np.int8)\n```\n\niii. The mapping order matches `TRIAL_OUTCOMES = ['hit', 'miss', 'false_alarm', 'correct_reject']`. The mapping is stored in `metadata['outcome_to_code']` for recovery.\n\n## 7-c. How is `output` *Trial outcome* aligned with the neural data?\n\nN/A — trial outcome is a per-trial constant, not a time-varying signal.\n\n## 8. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n\ni. Several cases are handled:\n- **Failed sessions**: If `extract_session_data` or `segment_trials` throws an exception, the session is skipped with a warning.\n- **Truncated trials**: If `stop_time` extends past the recording, the trial is clipped to the end. Trials with no frames (`end_idx <= start_idx`) are skipped.\n- **Missing behavioral data**: NaN values from interpolation (running speed or pupil diameter outside the recorded range) are mapped to bin 0 during discretization.\n- **Few trials**: Sessions with fewer than 2 valid trials are excluded.\n\nii.\n```python\ntry:\n    session_data = extract_session_data(bc, sess_exps)\n    trials = segment_trials(session_data)\nexcept Exception as e:\n    print(f'FAILED: {e}')\n    continue\n...\nif end_idx > T:\n    end_idx = T\nif end_idx <= start_idx:\n    continue\n...\nout[np.isnan(values)] = 0\n...\nif len(trials) < 2:\n    continue\n```\n\niii. The try/except ensures a single bad session doesn't crash the entire pipeline. NaN-to-0 mapping is a conservative default that avoids propagating missing data into the discretized output.\n\n## 9-a. What are the most time-consuming steps of the code?\n\ni. The most time-consuming step is loading each experiment via `bc.get_behavior_ophys_experiment()`, which downloads/reads large neural and behavioral data arrays from the S3 cache. This is I/O bound.\n\nii. N/A\n\niii. Each experiment contains full-session dF/F traces for all neurons, plus running speed, eye tracking, and trials data. The SDK caches files locally after first download, but reading them is still the bottleneck.\n\n## 9-b. What loops in the code could have been vectorized to improve efficiency?\n\ni. The per-trial loop in `segment_trials` iterates over each valid trial sequentially. The `np.searchsorted` and array slicing could theoretically be vectorized across all trials, but the loop is not a bottleneck compared to data loading.\n\nii. N/A\n\niii. The per-trial loop is simple and readable. Data loading dominates runtime, so vectorizing the trial loop would yield negligible speedup.\n\n## 9-c. What processing does the code repeat multiple times?\n\nNo processing is repeated. Each session is loaded once in Pass 1, and the extracted trial data is reused for discretization (bin edge computation) and final assembly without reloading.\n\n## 9-d. What unnecessary processing does the code do that is discarded in downstream analyses?\n\nN/A\n\n## 9-e. How is memory usage optimized?\n\ni. After trial extraction, the full-session arrays (`neural_data`, `running_speed`, `pupil_diameter`, `trials_table`) are dropped from memory. Only small metadata (`ophys_ts`, `plane_labels`) is retained for later use. The trial-level slices (already copied during `segment_trials`) are the only data kept.\n\nii.\n```python\nsession_meta = {\n    'ophys_ts': session_data['ophys_ts'],\n    'plane_labels': session_data['plane_labels'],\n}\ndel session_data\nsession_results.append((mouse_id, session_meta, trials))\n```\n\niii. The full-session `neural_data` array `(N_neurons, T)` is the largest object in memory. After `segment_trials` copies the per-trial slices, the full array is redundant. Dropping it immediately avoids accumulating all sessions' full arrays in memory simultaneously.\n\n","numLines":446,"startLine":1,"totalLines":446}}}

Determine trial outcome\n        outcome = 'other'\n        for label in TRIAL_OUTCOMES:\n            if row[label]:\n                outcome = label\n                break\n\n        # Time-varying image name: initial before change, change after\n        change_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts[idx], row['change_time'])\n        image_names = np.empty(n_frames, dtype=object)\n        image_names[:change_idx] = row['initial_image_name']\n        image_names[change_idx:] = row['change_image_name']\n\n        # NaN -> 0 (safest discrete label).\"\"\"\n    out = np.digitize(values, bin_edges[1:-1]).astype(np.int8)\n    out[np.isnan(values)] = 0\n    return out\n\n\ndef main():\n    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(\n        description='Convert Allen Visual Behavior Ophys data to pickle format')\n    parser.add_argument('output', help='Output pickle file path')\n    parser.add_argument('--datadir', type=str,\n                        default='/groups/zhang/home/zhangl5/Allen_Brain',\n                        help='Allen SDK cache directory (default: /groups/zhang/home/zhangl5/Allen_Brain)')\n    mode = parser.add_mutually_exclusive_group()\n    mode.add_argument('--full', action='store_true', default=True,\n                      help='Process all mice and sessions (default)')\n    mode.add_argument('--sample', action='store_true',\n                      help='Process only 2 mice with 2 sessions each for testing')\n    args = parser.parse_args()\n\n    t0 = time.time()\n    print(\"Loading Allen cache...\")\n    bc = get_cache(args.datadir)\n    experiment_table = bc.get_ophys_experiment_table()\n\n    #
